## TRA-Mention Flagging: Current Method vs Full-Text Search Capability

### 1. Current Method

**Local regex matching on cached filings.** Lines 24–26 of `/home/sulli/research/tra/scripts/tra_packet/sections.py`:

```python
def has_tra_mention(body: str) -&gt; bool:
    """Return True if the filing body mentions a tax receivable agreement."""
    return _TRA_PATTERN.search(body) is not None
```

The pattern at line 21 is: `r"(?i)tax\s+receivables?\s+agreements?"` (case-insensitive, whitespace-flexible, optional 's' plurals). This matches only the specific phrase "tax receivable agreement(s)" or "tax receivables agreement(s)"; it does NOT match "TRA" as a standalone acronym. 

The skill calls `has_tra_mention(body)` during mechanical assembly (SKILL.md line 141) after fetching each filing via `fetch_document()`. It flags the filing in the timeline with `tra_mention=yes/no` (SKILL.md line 92). **This is purely local; no SEC search is involved.**

### 2. Wrapper Capability

`search_filings()` (lines 97–107 in search.py) supports:

**(a) Phrase queries:** Yes. The `q` parameter is a free-form query string. SEC EDGAR full-text search uses Lucene syntax; double-quote for exact phrase: `'"tax receivable agreement"'` (documented in sec-edgar SKILL.md).

**(b) Per-CIK filtering:** Yes. The `ciks` parameter (line 102, lines 119–122) accepts a comma-separated list of zero-padded 10-digit CIKs. Server-side filtering.

**(c) Per-form filtering:** Yes. The `forms` parameter (line 99, lines 117–118) accepts comma-separated form types like `"8-K,10-K"`.

**(d) Date-range filtering:** Yes. Parameters `startdt` and `enddt` (lines 100–101, lines 123–128) in `"YYYY-MM-DD"` format with `dateRange="custom"`.

### 3. "TRA" Whole-Word Problem

The SEC EDGAR full-text search endpoint uses Lucene's standard tokenizer. A query for `TRA` will match the **token** "TRA", not substring matches inside words like "extract" or "ultra". To confirm: the endpoint tokenizes before indexing, so "extract" becomes the token `["extract"]`, and "TRA" becomes `["TRA"]`. A query for `TRA` returns only exact token matches.

**Practical verification:** The query `TRA` should work directly without post-filtering if the SEC's tokenization is standard. No Python regex word-boundary check is needed. However, if SEC's tokenizer has quirks (e.g., treating `"TRA"` inside hyphenated terms like `"TRA-related"` as a separate token), the wrapper does not defend against this. A safe downstream workaround would be to collect all SEC results, fetch the filing bodies, and apply a Python regex like `r"\bTRA\b"` to confirm true whole-word matches.

### 4. Cost and Rate

**Queries per firm:** The user's five phrase searches are:
1. `"tax receivable agreement"`
2. `"tax receivables agreement"`
3. `"tax receivable agreements"`
4. `"tax receivables agreements"`
5. Plus a sixth query `TRA` (whole-word)

Each query is independent and returns a separate LazyFrame. **Six queries per firm minimum**. If the results exceed 10,000 per query, pagination is automatic (lines 137–163: the while loop pages through results at PAGE_SIZE=100 until PAGE_CAP=10,000 or end-of-results). For a multi-firm run across 23 firms, that's **138 queries baseline** (6 × 23), but pagination adds sub-queries for large result sets. The meta dictionary (lines 175–182) flags `hit_cap=True` if 10,000 was reached.

**Rate limit:** SEC caps at 10 req/sec. 138 baseline queries at one query per second is well within budget. With pagination (estimate 20–30 sub-queries across all firms), still comfortably under the cap.

### 5. Feasibility Verdict

**Straightforward.** The wrapper is feature-complete. Integration requires:
1. Replace the local `has_tra_mention()` regex check with a call to `search_filings(q="...", ciks=cik_padded, forms="8-K,10-K,10-Q,DEF 14A", client=...)` for each of the five phrases plus "TRA".
2. Collect the returned LazyFrames into a merged result set.
3. Use the `adsh` column to mark filings with `tra_mention=True` in the filing timeline.

No new code needed beyond a wrapper function that loops over the five queries and aggregates results. The existing `EdgarClient`, caching, pagination, and CIK filtering handle all the complexity. **Estimate: &lt;50 lines of Python to integrate.**</result>

One design point: full-text search and local regex catch different things. Most robust is to keep both: full-text search as the discovery layer to know what to fetch, local regex as confirmation on the body. Or simpler: full-text search only. Preference?

2026-05-13T14:12:25-07:00(none)
User
why did the SEC block the user-agent?
Coauthor
The SEC's public policy for programmatic access to EDGAR (documented at `sec.gov/os/accessing-edgar-data`) requires every request to carry a `User-Agent` header in the format `<Name> <contact-email>`, so they can reach out if a user violates the 10-request-per-second rate cap or causes other issues. Requests without a compliant identifying header get blocked with a 403. WebFetch (Claude's web-fetch tool) does not let the agent set arbitrary headers, so its default User-Agent does not satisfy the policy. Our own `scripts/sec_edgar/search.py` does set a compliant header, which is why the existing wrapper works while WebFetch does not.

  - `scripts/sec_edgar/search.py` exposes `search_filings(q, forms, startdt, enddt, ciks, ...)` with a 10,000-hit cap and caches at `.tra_history_cache/edgar_search/`.
  - `scripts/sec_edgar/client.py` exposes `EdgarClient` with a compliant User-Agent and SEC's 10 req/sec rate limit baked in.
  - `scripts/sec_edgar/archives.py` likely has helpers for fetching filing-index documents and exhibits; read it before reinventing.
  - `scripts/sec_edgar/submissions.py` likely lists a firm's full filing history; read it.
  - You can run scripts via `cd /home/sulli/research/tra && PYTHONPATH=scripts pixi run python ...`.

A known wrapper gap: `search.py` does NOT retry on 5xx. The full-text-search endpoint occasionally returns HTTP 500. Add a local retry wrapper in your test script (1 to 2 retries with a 1.5s pause). Note this gap in your report; do NOT modify search.py itself in this dispatch (that's a separate decision).

==== Sampling method ====

Use the SEC full-index files at `https://www.sec.gov/Archives/edgar/full-index/<YYYY>/QTRn/form.idx` to get an unbiased list of all 10-K and 8-K filings per year/quarter. Format is fixed-width text; parse with a regex or read documentation if needed. (Fetch with `EdgarClient` so the User-Agent is compliant.)

For each year 2001 through 2007:
  - Pool all 10-Ks filed in that year across the four quarters.
  - Pool all 8-Ks filed in that year across the four quarters.
  - Random-sample 5 from each pool.

Random seed: derive from today (2026-05-13). Use `random.seed(20260513)` so the sample is reproducible.

==== Per-filing test ====

For each sampled filing:

1. Build the archive URL `https://www.sec.gov/Archives/edgar/data/<CIK_int>/<accession_no_dashes>/`.
2. Fetch the filing index (index.json or filename listing). Identify the primary document plus all exhibits (files matching `ex-?\d+` patterns, plus the primary doc).
3. Download the primary doc and exhibits to `coauthor/2026-05-12-edgar-scrape/findings/sec-index/<YYYY>-<FORM>/<CIK_padded>/<accession_no_dashes>/`.
   - Use `<FORM>` literal `10K` or `8K` (no slash, no dash).
   - Cache hits should be reused.
4. From each downloaded document, extract a distinctive phrase using the same methodology as the prior test in this session:
   - Strip HTML tags (regex; no bs4 needed).
   - Find sentence-like chunks (80 to 300 chars, no ALL-CAPS tokens, no $amounts, no `Section N` refs).
   - From the middle sentence, take 6 to 7 contiguous words verbatim. Skip any phrase containing an all-caps 2+ char token (excludes "TRA", "IRS", etc.).
5. Query `search_filings(q=f'"{phrase}"', ciks=cik_padded, startdt=<filing_date - 180d>, enddt=<filing_date + 180d>)`.
6. Check if the filing's accession is in the returned `adsh` column.
7. Status per filing: `INDEXED_BODY` if the primary doc phrase recovered the accession; `INDEXED_EXHIBIT` if at least one exhibit phrase did; `NOT_FOUND` if neither; `ERROR` if extraction or query failed.

A filing counts as a clear success if EITHER the primary doc OR any exhibit recovers the accession. Track both signals separately so we know whether the body is consistently indexed vs whether exhibits are.

If a sampled filing has no extractable phrase (too short, all numbers, etc.), record that and skip; do not let it crash the run.

==== Deliverables ====

1. The downloaded documents under `coauthor/2026-05-12-edgar-scrape/findings/sec-index/<YYYY>-<FORM>/<CIK>/`.
2. A summary written to `coauthor/2026-05-12-edgar-scrape/findings/sec-index/SUMMARY.md`:
   - One-paragraph overview of method and seed.
   - Headline counts: total tested, INDEXED, NOT_FOUND, ERROR, split by year and form.
   - A table: year, form, firms-sampled, body-indexed, any-exhibit-indexed, not-found, errors.
   - A short "Outliers" section flagging any NOT_FOUND cases with the firm name, CIK, accession, and the phrase that failed (so a human can spot-check the SEC search UI manually).
3. A brief inline report under 400 words: top-line verdict, table, list of caveats including the 5xx retry gap.

==== Out of scope ====

- Do not modify `scripts/sec_edgar/search.py` (the 5xx retry gap stays in the report, not in code).
- Do not write a separate fix-the-wrapper PR.
- Do not try to interpret what the indexed filings contain; just verify whether the phrase recovers the accession.

==== Cost estimate ====

~70 filings × ~5 HTTP fetches each + ~140 search queries = ~500 HTTP calls. At SEC's 10 req/sec budget, the rate-limited wall time should be ~1 minute plus per-call overhead. Wall-time probably 5 to 15 minutes total.

Coauthor
The structure makes sense and I agree with the parallelization rationale. Skills 2 and 3 should be separate, for four reasons:

1. **Different abstraction levels.** Skill 2 operates per-filing (small, repetitive); Skill 3 operates per-firm (synthesis). The per-filing annotations are a useful primary artifact, not just intermediate state for timeline construction.

2. **Reusability for the eventual database.** When the timeline format evolves (it will, as you scale to a database with regression-grade fields), you do not want to re-read every filing. Per-filing annotations cached as a primary deliverable let Skill 3 iterate freely.

3. **Parallelization profile.** Skill 2 parallelizes across filings within a firm; Skill 3 is per-firm serial synthesis. Different bottlenecks, different agent dispatch patterns.

4. **Failure isolation.** If a timeline pass goes wrong or you want a different framing, Skill 2's slow agent-driven contract identification stays intact.

The cost of the split is one extra handoff between skills, but the handoff is natural (Skill 2 emits a structured per-filing annotation log; Skill 3 reads it).

**Critical S-1/S-1A fix to flag for emphasis:** the current skill keeps only the earliest S-1, which is wrong. A firm can announce a TRA in an early S-1, scrap it in a later S-1/A, and the absence of a TRA in the final S-1 is real signal that the current skill discards. Skill 1 must collect every S-1 and every S-1/A version.

A few clarifying questions before I sketch the SKILL.md skeletons:

1. **"Render the file" for contract identification.** What does this look like operationally? Options: (a) strip HTML to readable text and feed that to the agent; (b) convert to PDF and feed image pages; (c) the agent loads the raw HTML and tolerates the noise. The first is cheap and probably 80% of the value; the second is more faithful for tables but slower and more expensive; the third is fragile. Preference?

2. **Skill 3 machine-readable output.** Since this is the backbone of a future database, should Skill 3 also emit a structured artifact (JSON or parquet with timeline events as rows) alongside the human-readable narrative? Easy to add now, painful to retrofit.

3. **Form list for the search filter (Skill 1).** You listed 10-K, 10-Q, "international versions", DEF 14A and similar, and 8-K. To pin down: 20-F, 40-F, 6-K (foreign analogues); DEF 14A, DEFA14A, DEFM14A, PRE 14A (proxy variants); 10-K/A, 10-Q/A, 8-K/A (amendments). Anything else? Do the existing-skill forms (S-1, S-1/A, S-4, S-4/A, 424B prospectuses) get the same text-search treatment, or stay fetched by enumeration as today?

a5a2d47b69921fc59SendMessage2026-05-13T16:56:24-07:00
Prompt
Tightening the retry-wrapper code snippet in `~/.claude/skills/tra-download-filings/SKILL.md`. Replace the current broad-except/type-name-string-matching version with a correct one using `httpx.HTTPStatusError.response.status_code`.

Correct snippet:

```python
import time
import httpx

def search_with_retry(*args, max_attempts: int = 3, backoff_s: float = 1.5, **kwargs):
    for attempt in range(max_attempts):
        try:
            return search_filings(*args, **kwargs)
        except httpx.HTTPStatusError as e:
            if e.response.status_code >= 500 and attempt < max_attempts - 1:
                time.sleep(backoff_s)
                continue
            raise
```

Drop any prior "imprecise; a coder implementing the script should use ..." comment, since the snippet is now correct. Keep the surrounding prose explaining why retry is needed (search.py 5xx gap) intact.

Report back when done. Under 50 words.

**Diagnosis.** SEC's full-text-search `forms` parameter has a parser quirk: comma-separated lists that include slash-bearing form codes (10-K/A, 10-Q/A, 8-K/A) return ~0 hits or arbitrary partial counts. Adding "10-K/A" to a single "10-K" query drops the total from 16 to 0. The 22-form list in the test returned only 10 hits because the slash-bearing entries broke the parsing.

Prompt
Question about Claude Code's auto-compaction behavior, specifically how it interacts with skill content vs file content.

When the model approaches the context-window limit, Claude Code performs automatic compaction (summarization of older messages). I need to know precisely:

1. **Skill content via the Skill tool.** When a user (or the model) invokes a skill via the Skill tool, the SKILL.md content is loaded into the assistant's context. Is that content treated specially during auto-compaction? Specifically:
   - Is it pinned (never summarized)?
   - Is it injected into the system prompt or an equivalently durable position?
   - Or is it appended as a regular message that the compactor can summarize like any other content?

2. **File content via the Read tool.** When the assistant uses the Read tool to read a file (including a SKILL.md file at `~/.claude/skills/<name>/SKILL.md`), the file content arrives as a tool-result message. Is that content treated as ordinary conversation history and therefore subject to summarization during compaction? Or is there any preservation logic?

3. **Practical implication.** If a subagent is dispatched with a prompt that says "Read ~/.claude/skills/X/SKILL.md and follow the workflow there", and the subagent's work later triggers auto-compaction, would the SKILL.md content be at risk of being summarized away, leaving the subagent without the original instructions? Compare with a direct skill invocation via the Skill tool.

4. **Frontmatter directives.** When a SKILL.md has frontmatter fields like `model:` or `allowed-tools:`, are those directives applied only when the skill is invoked via the Skill tool, or also when the file is loaded via Read?

This matters for a real test that just failed. We dispatched a subagent with "read SKILL.md and follow it" for a task that involves 146 documents, and the subagent appears to have shortcutted the spec. We're trying to figure out whether context-pressure-driven loss of the skill's instructions is a likely contributor.

Cite Claude Code docs or source where possible. If something is undocumented, say so explicitly rather than guessing. Report under 350 words.
Response
Coauthor
Real question and I don't have confidence in the answer. My priors are that skill content invoked via the Skill tool may be pinned (like system-prompt material) while file content read via the Read tool is treated as ordinary tool-result messages and is fair game for compaction, but I have not verified either claim. If that asymmetry is real, your hypothesis is a strong contributor.

Dispatching claude-code-guide to confirm the actual mechanics.

Confirmed by claude-code-guide. Your hypothesis was right:

- **Read-via-Read**: SKILL.md lands as a tool-result message, fully compactable, no preservation logic. Frontmatter directives (`model:`, `allowed-tools:`) are bypassed entirely.
- **Skill tool invocation**: content is compactable, but Claude Code re-attaches the first 5,000 tokens of each invoked skill after summarization, within a 25K combined budget across all re-attached skills. Frontmatter directives are honored.

For the 146-document dispatch: if compaction fired (very plausible at that document count), the spec was at risk of being summarized away, and the analyst's defaults plus shortcuts took over. Combined with the haiku-vs-opus issue, that is enough to explain the failure.

Practical fixes:

1. Invocation pattern: slash-command invocation rather than Read-and-follow.
2. Frontmatter on the SKILL.md: `model: opus-4-7` (force the right model when invoked) and `context: fork` per the guide's suggestion (gives the skill an isolated context window; the guide mentioned this but I have not independently verified the syntax, so worth confirming before relying on it).
3. Decompose the work into smaller batches that stay under context limits.

Path forward: add the frontmatter, re-dispatch as slash-command invocation or as an Agent call with explicit `model: "opus"` override, on a focused document subset.

Two ways to close the gap:

1. **Always pull all 8-Ks for the firm regardless of keyword match.** 8-Ks are short and few per year; cost is modest. Adds a second "completeness pass" to the download skill for the 8-K form.
2. **Expand the search keywords.** Add phrases like "Chapter 11", "Item 1.03", "rejection of executory contracts", "wind-down" to catch event-driven filings. More fragile; the keyword list keeps growing.

Coauthor
90 filings downloaded, 72 MiB. Events query caught the merger-agreement announcement 8-K, the proxy materials, and the December 2022 closing-period exhibit. Good coverage for an M&A-close termination test. Dispatching tra-processor.

Strong run. Substantive findings:

**Switch TRA, resolved:**
- Originated 2017-10-05 at IPO (85% rate, Up-C structure).
- Liability grew from $52.5M initial recognition (12/31/2018) to $412.5M by Q1 2022.
- **May 11, 2022:** Amendment No. 1 executed concurrent with the merger agreement, under Section 7.6(b) of the original TRA. Section 2 terminates the TRA effective May 11, 2022; Section 3 substitutes a fixed $0.37/Common Unit cash payment payable on Change-of-Control close or December 31, 2022 (whichever earlier).
- Q2 2022 10-Q recognized $372.8M gain on termination; liability remeasured from $412.5M to $75.1M (the cap, ~19% of unamended balance).

**The 2018 deferred-pipeline cancel-signal is the third systematic false positive across our three test firms** (Vince: 2018 covenant restriction; SmileDirectClub: 2021 valuation allowance; Switch: 2018 initial liability recognition). The pipeline mis-reads various "TRA liability moved" patterns as "TRA cancelled". Worth noting for the larger project.

**Spec ambiguity surfaced — cap-and-release amendments.** Switch's mechanism is a hybrid: a real on-EDGAR amendment (mechanism 2 in the spec) but the cash payment is contingent on an extrinsic event (mechanism 3, the M&A close), and the payment is a negotiated cap rather than the contract's Section 4.01 formula. The agent tagged it `terminated_by_amendment` and noted the M&A contingency, but the spec could explicitly recognize cap-and-release as a distinct pattern.

This is actually how most going-private transactions with TRAs resolve in practice: the acquirer's special committee negotiates a cap, the amendment is executed at signing as a merger pre-condition. Worth capturing.

Capture the cap-and-release pattern in the spec, or move to the next firm?

## Project summary across all 26 firms

**13 distinct termination/status patterns observed:**

1. Expiration by terms (Vince, WEX)
2. Cap-and-release at M&A signing (Switch, Vertiv, VWR Corp, McAfee, Evo, Enfusion; pending: CC Neuberger)
3. Standalone Payment-and-Termination Agreement (Sabre, Fifth Street)
4. Acquirer extinguishment (Comcast → DreamWorks's TRA, $146M at close)
5. Bankruptcy rejection (Cano explicit; Jones Energy explicit; SmileDirectClub and DMS unverified)
6. Beneficiary reassignment (Acreage → Canopy USA, no termination)
7. Off-ledger transfer (Oaktree → Brookfield, deconsolidated)
8. Economically extinguished but legally alive (FXCM, RSA reinstates)
9. Active with full valuation allowance (BRC, MSP Recovery)
10. Active and paying / amended on schedule (Apollo, Surgery Partners)
11. Beneficiary-side (firm receives TRA payments — McKesson)
12. No TRA under firm's CIK (VWR Funding — financing-arm pattern)
13. Never executed (Swiftmerge/AleAnna — drafted then removed pre-close)

**Cancel-signal accuracy:** of the 26 deferred-CSV cancel-signals, the great majority were false positives or misaligned. The most common failure modes were the initial-recognition signal (signal date = first 10-Q after TRA creation), the valuation-allowance disclosure, the covenant-restriction misread, and the cash-flow line item "Satisfaction of tax receivable agreement" misread as termination.

**New spec ambiguities (worth a future capture pass):**

- "Repurchase Agreement" / "Payment and Termination Agreement" / "Waiver and Termination" titles outside the original taxonomy (Sabre, Fifth Street, Vertiv, WEX)
- "Waiver of early termination" instrument (Apollo May 2022)
- Beneficiary-side processing (McKesson)
- Acquirer-side processing (Comcast)
- Financing-arm with no TRA (VWR Funding)
- `never_executed` status (Swiftmerge)
- Off-ledger / deconsolidated transfer (Oaktree)
- Form-of-TRA embedded in S-4 narrative rather than as discrete exhibit
- Annex A redaction of per-recipient amounts (Evo Payments)
- TRA Bonus Plans as parallel non-TRA instruments (Acreage)
- Beneficiary trusts added at execution (Fifth Street)
- Beneficiary reassignment without termination (Acreage → Canopy USA)
- RSA reinstating a TRA through bankruptcy (FXCM)

coauthor_coderAgent2026-05-14T00:39:22-07:00
Prompt
Build a master list of all SEC-filer CIKs that have ever appeared in a filing mentioning a TRA. Use the SEC full-text search ONLY. Do NOT touch the `<period>_notes*.parquet` files; reading those eagerly has crashed WSL before.

==== Approach ====

Use `scripts/sec_edgar/search.py`. The wrapper exposes `search_filings_paginated_by_date(...)` per its docstring (splits date range recursively when a query exceeds 10K hits). Run two queries spanning 2001-01-01 through today:

1. Phrase-OR: `'"tax receivable agreement" OR "tax receivable agreements" OR "tax receivables agreement" OR "tax receivables agreements"'`
2. Token: `'TRA'`

No `ciks` filter, no `forms` filter (the omit-forms-and-post-filter pattern). Wrap with the 5xx-retry helper.

From the returned LazyFrames, aggregate distinct CIKs from the `ciks` column (note: it is a list per row, since some filings are filed jointly by multiple registrants — flatten and unique). Three options:

1. **Sample first.** Random 50 CIKs, run end-to-end, measure obligor rate. If it's ~30%, projected obligor universe is ~700-800 and the full run is more tractable. Cheap and informative.
2. **Cheap pre-screen via exhibit-title check.** For each CIK, fetch the filings index (one lightweight request per registration-class filing) and check exhibit descriptions for "tax receivable". Only firms with a TRA-titled exhibit get the full download + process. Filters aggressively; bigger upfront work than the sample but bounds total cost.

**Sanity-check (10-K, sampled with seed = today's date)**:

Body samples: `goco-20201231.htm`, `mnq4201210-k.htm`, **`pzn201710kex1035.htm`** (FN), **`pzn-ex1022_211.htm`** (FN), `gsky10k2018.htm`, `file1.htm`, `mck10k3312020.htm`, `d482186d10k.htm`, `d31834d10k.htm`, `loco-10k12262018.htm`.

**Heuristic weakness**: the `^ex…` anchor misses exhibit filenames where `ex` is preceded by other tokens (`pzn201710kex1035.htm`, `pzn-ex1022_211.htm`). Two of ten body samples were false negatives. The true body counts are therefore modest overestimates; the 10-K body-only count of 1,758 likely has ~10-20% noise (rough extrapolation from 2/10 in the spot check). A tighter rule would be `(?:^|[-_./])ex[-_\.]?\d` — anchoring `ex` to start-of-string OR a word-boundary punctuation. The relative comparison (10-K mostly body, 8-K mostly exhibit) is unaffected.

**Headline finding**: 10-K and 10-Q phrase-OR matches are overwhelmingly in the filing body (95-99% of accessions). 8-K matches flip the other way — only 40% of 8-K phrase-OR hits have a body match; 60% are exhibit-only. That fits the institutional pattern: 8-Ks announce a TRA event with the agreement itself attached as Exhibit 10.x, while 10-K/10-Q narrative discloses TRA balances and activity inline.

The big finding: only **335 CIKs ever had a 10-K body mention** of the TRA phrase. The other 1,445 CIKs in the allow-list-filtered phrase-OR set never narrated about a TRA in their annual report. That's a strong filter for actual TRA bearers vs mention-only filers.

**8-K body 60% vs exhibit 60%** flip confirms the institutional pattern: 8-Ks announce a TRA via Item 1.01 with the agreement attached as Exhibit 10.X; 10-K/10-Q bodies narrate balances and activity inline.

Heuristic caveat: the `^ex` anchor misses exhibit filenames where `ex` is preceded by other tokens (`pzn201710kex1035.htm`). Two of ten body samples were false negatives. True body counts are ~10-20% overestimated. Tightening the rule to `(?:^|[-_./])ex[-_\.]?\d` would fix it. The proportions are still correct.

**Implications for scope.**

Cleaner candidate universes:
- **335 CIKs:** ever had a 10-K body TRA mention. Strongest signal for TRA obligor.
- **Union of {10-K body, 10-Q body, 8-K body, 8-K exhibit} CIKs:** probably 450-550 distinct firms. Add 10-Q since not every firm files a 10-K body mention but their 10-Qs do; add 8-K exhibit since IPO-close 8-Ks attach the actual TRA.

2026-05-15T10:23:07-07:00(none)
User
the contracts being saved to /contracts/ are only TRAs right?
Coauthor
Yes — the `contracts/` directory is reserved for documents in the TRA's lifecycle only. That means:

- Original TRA (executed or unexecuted form-of)
- Amended and Restated TRA
- Numbered amendments to the TRA
- Termination instruments (standalone termination doc, or the cap-and-release / repurchase / payment-and-termination instruments that effect termination)
- Beneficiary-modification instruments tied to the TRA (waiver and assignment, prepayment agreement, ratification of partial buyout)

Unrelated contracts that often appear alongside the TRA in the same filing (credit agreements, indenture, exchange agreement, stockholders agreement, registration rights agreement, LLC operating agreement, executive employment agreements, etc.) are NOT saved here. The spec lists these explicitly under "What is NOT a TRA". A parallel "TRA Bonus Plan" (compensation arrangement that pays employees a share of TRA savings as a bonus — observed at Acreage Holdings) is also kept out of `contracts/` and only annotated as a related-but-separate instrument in `contract_log.md`.

So yes — every file under `contracts/` should be a TRA-lifecycle document, just under a broader reading of "TRA" than the literal word "Tax Receivable Agreement" in the title.

2026-05-15T14:15:57-07:00(none)
User
what do you mean by the TRAs being "bilateral vs multilateral"
Coauthor
Bilateral = two-party contract: PubCo (obligor) plus one beneficiary group. Three of the 2012 Vantiv TRAs are bilateral: one with Fifth Third, one with Advent, one with JPDN. Each is a separate contract document with two named parties.

Multilateral = three-or-more-party contract: PubCo plus multiple distinct beneficiary groups, all named as parties in one document. The 2012 NPC NOLs TRA is multilateral: Vantiv plus Fifth Third plus Advent plus JPDN, all parties to the same instrument. The contract has to specify how the 85% gets allocated among the three beneficiary groups (typically by their pre-IPO ownership shares of the tax attributes), and any payment cycle covers all of them.

Practical consequence visible in Vantiv's history: when Advent and JPDN were bought out in October 2013, each Termination Agreement had to release that party from BOTH its bilateral TRA AND its share of the multilateral NPC NOLs TRA. So Advent's $112M termination payment covered the Advent bilateral plus Advent's share of NPC NOLs; same for JPDN's $0.5M. Fifth Third's share of NPC NOLs persisted alongside the Fifth Third bilateral.

If the NPC NOLs had been structured as three separate bilateral TRAs (one each with Fifth Third, Advent, JPDN), the legal mechanics would be the same but you'd have five 2012 contracts (4 step-up + 3 NOL = 7) instead of four (4 step-up + 1 multilateral NOL). Vantiv chose the multilateral structure presumably because the NPC NOLs are a shared pool and the inter-beneficiary allocation is cleaner in one document.
2026-05-15T14:22:49-07:00(none)
User
this language doesn't come up in any other documentation right? you're just using it ad-hoc?
Coauthor
Yes, ad-hoc. "Bilateral" and "multilateral" are general contract-law terms I borrowed to describe the two-party-vs-many-party distinction. The actual TRA documents and 10-K disclosures don't use those labels — they just name the parties and describe the tax-attribute coverage. I used the shorthand to make the contrast quick to read, but it isn't standard TRA terminology and I should have flagged that when I introduced it.

Plain-language equivalents:

- "Bilateral" → just "with Fifth Third" or "two-party TRA"
- "Multilateral" → "joint TRA among multiple beneficiary groups" or "three-party TRA"

Should I restate the Vantiv summary without those terms?
2026-05-15T14:25:30-07:00(none)
User
not the inline summary, but any summary in a saved file should avoid these terms. The reason is that it's not a useful distinction: most often the TRA will not specifically name the counterparty or not name all of the counterparties, instead describing them as the holders of the llc shares. In this sense there are only 2 parties in any TRA: the company and the llc shareholders. My concern about the use of "multilateral" was about it sounding like there is something going on between all three parties, rather than it being a single relationship, but breaking down who the llc shareholders are. Hope that helps explain my question

coauthor_analystAgent2026-05-15T15:18:54-07:00
Prompt
Replace the phrase "cap-and-release" (and the flag name `cap_and_release: true`) across all saved analysis files and the SKILL.md. The user's clarification: "cap" misleadingly implies that future payments may still occur but are limited in amount, whereas what is actually happening is a single payment now that ends the contract.

==== Replacement rules ====

Choose the contextually appropriate term:

- **"termination payment"** — basic case where a single payment ends the contract.
- **"CoC payment"** — when the payment is triggered by a Change of Control event (Section 4.01 / typical M&A).
- **"settlement payment"** — when negotiated outside the contract's own early-termination formula.
- **"negotiated termination payment"** — when the payment is materially less than the contractually defined Early Termination Payment formula (i.e., the parties negotiated a discount).

Use the descriptive language; if a sentence uses "cap-and-release sub-pattern" as a sub-section name, rewrite it as "Negotiated termination payment sub-pattern".

For the flag `cap_and_release: true`, rename to `negotiated_termination_payment: true` (the most general appropriate label).

==== Files to scrub ====

1. All `contract_log.md` and `filing_notes.md` files under `/home/sulli/research/tra/coauthor/2026-05-12-edgar-scrape/findings/test-run/*/`.
2. `/home/sulli/.claude/skills/tra-process-filings/SKILL.md`. After replacing the in-body usage, also add a forbid-the-term entry near the top (under "Universal constraints" next to the bilateral/multilateral rule):

> "Do not use the phrase 'cap-and-release' to describe a TRA termination. 'Cap' implies that further payments may still occur subject to a ceiling, when in fact a single payment terminates the contract. Use 'termination payment' (basic), 'CoC payment' (Change of Control-triggered), 'settlement payment' (negotiated outside the contract's ETP formula), or 'negotiated termination payment' (negotiated for materially less than the contract's ETP). The status flag was renamed from `cap_and_release` to `negotiated_termination_payment`."

==== Report under 200 words ====

- Files edited with replacement counts per file.
- A sample before/after for one substantive replacement so I can verify the contextual choice was sensible.
- Confirmation that the SKILL.md rule was added with its line range.
- Any case where the replacement context was ambiguous (e.g., a sentence using "cap-and-release" generally rather than in a specific Section 4.01 context).

2026-05-15T15:29:38-07:00(none)
User
but there is a contingency here right? the first payment can be higher than the 650M if the tax benefits exceed this right? It's just that the second payment will then be lower. But this is a higher fair value
Coauthor
You're right and I underspecified that. Total payment is fixed at $1.35B, but timing is contingent on 2020 Wildfire Claim Tax Benefits, and that timing contingency does shift fair value.

The realized payments confirm it: the agent's report stated both "2021 Payment is the lesser of $650M and 2020 Wildfire Claim Tax Benefits" AND "the 2021 payment of $758M exceeded the $650M floor". Those two statements are contradictory; the realized $758M proves the first phrasing was wrong. The actual structure is:

- 2021 Payment ≥ $650M (floor, not cap)
- 2021 Payment can be higher up to the realized 2020 Wildfire Claim Tax Benefits (per the contract's $758M actual)
- 2022 Payment = $1.35B − 2021 Payment

So the contingency works as you described: higher 2020 Wildfire Claim Tax Benefits → higher 2021 Payment → lower 2022 Payment → same $1.35B total, but accelerated. The Trust benefits from any acceleration (higher present value); they don't lose anything from delay since the full $1.35B is contractually owed by the January 2022 backstop date regardless.

So "fixed" was the wrong characterization. More accurately: aggregate payment is fixed at $1.35B; the timing split between the 2021 and 2022 tranches is contingent on the Utility's actual 2020 tax-benefit realization, with the 2021 tranche having a $650M floor and the 2022 tranche being the residual. Fair value at signing depended on the Utility's projected 2020 tax-benefit realization (the higher the expected 2020 benefits, the more the value of the Trust's claim was front-loaded).

Want me to have the analyst update PG&E's contract_log.md with this corrected characterization?

quick question: are the subdirectories being cleaned up by the tra processing skill?
Coauthor
No. The tra-processor skill writes `contract_log.md`, `filing_notes.md`, and the `TRA-*/` directory, but it does NOT delete the raw `<accession>/` subdirectories from the download. So after processing, each firm directory has both: the analysis outputs (small) plus the raw filings (large).

That's why the original 26 firms needed a separate manual cleanup pass (the "delete raw filings, keep analyses" dispatch earlier this session). For the current 751-firm run, the same situation applies: every processed firm will accumulate ~50-200 MB of raw filings on disk until a separate cleanup runs.

Two ways to handle it:

1. **Add cleanup as a final step in the tra-processor skill.** After writing `contract_log.md`, `filing_notes.md`, and the `TRA-*/` directory, delete the `<accession>/` subdirectories. Permanent but irreversible — if you re-read or re-process the firm later, the raw documents are gone (would need re-download).

2. **Keep cleanup as a separate post-pass.** Same pattern as the earlier 26-firm pass. Run it after both the download and processing are confirmed correct for the entire batch. Reversible until invoked.

2026-05-15T15:35:19-07:00(none)
User
whats going to be in the final skill?
Coauthor
Per the SKILL.md output spec:

```
<firm_dir>/
├── TRA-<origination-date>[-<differentiator>]/
│   ├── <version-1>.<ext>
│   ├── <version-2>.<ext>
│   └── termination-<date>.<ext>      (if applicable)
├── contract_log.md
└── filing_notes.md
```

After cleanup (whether built into the skill or a separate post-pass), each firm directory carries only those three things: the per-firm TRA contract subdirectories with the actual TRA-lifecycle documents, the contract-identification rationale, and the per-filing annotations.

A typical TRA-bearing firm might end up with one TRA directory and the two markdown files; a firm with multiple TRAs (like Worldpay with five) has multiple TRA subdirectories. A non-obligor firm (Beneficiary / Acquirer / Financing arm / mention-only / `never_executed`) ends up with no TRA subdirectories — just `contract_log.md` (recording the role determination and any cross-CIK references) and `filing_notes.md`.

Totals are small: a few KB per markdown file plus typically 100 KB to a few MB per saved TRA contract HTML. Per firm, an active-TRA case ends around 0.5 to 5 MB; a non-obligor case is under 50 KB.

Compare to the pre-cleanup state where each firm dir also carries the raw `<accession>/` subdirectories from the download — roughly 50 to 500 MB per firm.

User
ok, the process shut down so maybe 20 is too many. How much context do these agents have at the end of a run. Can we have an agent do multiple of them?
Coauthor
Honest answers:

**1. Each agent ends a run with significant context.** A tra-processor agent loads the `tra-process-filings` skill (~5K tokens) plus my dispatch prompt at start, then accumulates the SKILL.md, filings (HTML-stripped), and its own writes. For a small Realogy guarantor with 2 filings, that's maybe 20-30K tokens at end-of-run. For a large firm like Avis Budget (274 filings), Charter (407 filings), or Cloud Peak (172 filings), the run-end context can easily reach 150-200K tokens. So a fresh agent per firm is the safe default for substantive firms.

**2. Yes, batching is feasible — but only for the small-firm cases.** I can dispatch one agent with a prompt enumerating multiple firms, telling it to process them in sequence. The good fit: **Realogy guarantor subsidiaries**, which are all 2-25 filings, all the same pattern, and the agent's accumulated state from firm 1 is genuinely useful when processing firm 2 (it already knows the WEX/Cendant cross-CIK reference structure, the canonical omission sentence, etc.). For these, one agent could plausibly do 5-10 in a row.

The trade-off:
- Pro: ~5x reduction in my dispatch overhead, ~5x reduction in parallel-agent pressure on the harness
- Con: if any firm in the batch crashes the whole batch may stall; can't parallelize within the batch
- The crash itself: most likely cause is WSL2 memory pressure on your local machine from 20 parallel background agent processes plus the parent harness, not anything Anthropic-side

**My recommendation**: I'll batch the remaining ~40+ Realogy guarantor subsidiaries (3-5 firms per agent) and keep single-firm dispatch for substantive cases. That should let us push throughput while keeping crash blast radius small.

Let me first check the current state, then proceed.

Signify Health (CIK 0001828182) is the **PubCo (TRA obligor)** in an Up-C structure with Cure TopCo, LLC as the operating partnership. **One TRA** was identified, originated 2021-02-12 at the IPO, with three saved contract files in `TRA-2021-02-12/`: the form-of (unexecuted) TRA filed as Exhibit 10.4 to the 2021-02-02 S-1/A, the executed counterpart filed as Exhibit 10.5 to the 2021-03-25 FY2020 10-K, and the 2022-09-02 terminating amendment filed as Exhibit 99.2 to the 2022-09-06 merger-announcement 8-K. Status flag transitions from `in_force` to `terminated_merger` on 2023-03-29 at the CVS Pharmacy merger close. The unusual feature is the **zero-payment termination structure**: the September 2022 amendment extinguished the TRA contingent on the merger close with no consideration to TRA Parties, which is materially different from the more common negotiated-termination-payment pattern seen at peer Up-C going-private deals (Switch, Vertiv, VWR, McAfee, Evo, Enfusion all paid something); the proxy materials note that New Mountain's affiliate (the controlling TRA beneficiary and 54.4% Class A / 74.7% Class B stockholder) effectively traded its TRA-payment rights for merger consideration on its equity, and Goldman Sachs's October 2022 supplemental proxy disclosure put the present value of the Signify-side tax benefit net of the TRA liability at only $14.5M as of June 30, 2022 against a balance-sheet TRA liability of $56.3M-$59.1M, suggesting the TRA-net-of-tax-benefits position was relatively small relative to the deal size. ## Status summary

Redbox Entertainment Inc. (CIK 0001820201) is the **PubCo (TRA obligor)** under one TRA originated October 22, 2021 at the close of the SPAC-to-Up-C business combination between Seaport Global Acquisition Corp. and Redwood Holdco, LP / Redwood Intermediate, LLC, 85% payout on Section 754 basis step-ups and tax-attribute pickups from exchanges. **One TRA, final status `terminated_by_amendment` (effective August 11, 2022, no payment).** The termination chain runs through three TRA-affecting instruments saved alongside the original contract in `/home/sulli/research/tra/coauthor/2026-05-12-edgar-scrape/findings/test-run/redbox-entertainment-inc_0001820201/TRA-2021-10-22/`: the original October 22, 2021 executed TRA, the April 15, 2022 Voting and Support Agreement (Section 2 conditionally commits Redwood and Redbox to terminate the TRA on close of a Board-approved strategic Transaction), the April 15, 2022 Waiver Under TRA (permanently waives Section 4.1(c) Change of Control acceleration only for the board-composition trigger), and the May 10, 2022 TRA Amendment (terminates the TRA immediately prior to the Effective Time of the Chicken Soup for the Soul merger, with Redwood Holdco voluntarily forewenting any payment). The merger closed August 11, 2022, effecting termination. Unusual notes: the termination is a **zero-consideration** Mechanism 2 amendment — the TRA Holder explicitly forewent "substantial economic, financial and pecuniary benefits" without any settlement payment, which is unusual for a CSSE-type merger close (most peer firms negotiated a fixed payment); the practical explanation is that the underlying deferred tax assets carried a full valuation allowance and the TRA liability was "not deemed probable" throughout the contract's life (no accrual ever recognized), so the Holder had no realistic prospect of recovery anyway.

2026-05-16T17:04:32-07:00(none)
User
it crashed again. any idea why it happens so much?
Coauthor
Best guess is WSL2 memory pressure. Each tra-processor pulls in dozens to hundreds of HTML filings, and several of those (10-Ks, DEFM14A proxies, S-4s) are multi-megabyte documents the agent reads end-to-end. With 5 parallel agents simultaneously holding 100-150k tokens of filing text in their working context plus the harness tracking all five in-flight tool streams, peak memory spikes well past what WSL2's default ~50% host-RAM cap comfortably absorbs. The wave 47 and wave 52 crashes both hit during long-duration agents (500k+ ms) that were processing CIKs with 200+ filings (EFH cluster, Forterra, Fortress), which lines up with the memory-pressure story.

Three things you could do if it keeps happening:

1. Bump WSL2's memory ceiling in `~/.wslconfig` (`memory=24GB` or whatever your host can spare). Default is 8GB on many systems.
2. Drop to 3 parallel agents during waves heavy on big firms (financial conglomerates, post-merger PubCos with 200+ filings). The throughput cost is small versus a full crash that requires checking partials.
3. Pre-screen firm directories by file count and split very-large firms (250+ filings) into their own single-agent waves.

Want me to drop to 3-agent waves going forward, or stick with 5 and accept the occasional crash?
2026-05-16T17:05:44-07:00(none)
User
I dont have a wslconfig file, how do I set it up
Coauthor
The file lives on the Windows side, not in WSL. From WSL it's accessible at `/mnt/c/Users/<your-windows-username>/.wslconfig`.

Steps:

1. Find your Windows username (from WSL): `ls /mnt/c/Users/` — pick the one that isn't `Public`, `Default`, or `All Users`.

2. Create the file at that path with contents like:

```
[wsl2]
memory=24GB
swap=8GB
processors=8
```

Pick `memory` based on how much RAM the host has (leave 8GB or so for Windows itself). `processors` defaults to all cores; setting it explicitly is optional. `swap` gives the kernel headroom if peak memory briefly exceeds the cap.

3. From Windows PowerShell or CMD (not WSL), run `wsl --shutdown`. Wait ~10 seconds, then reopen your WSL terminal. The new limits take effect on next start.

4. Verify from WSL: `free -h` — the "Mem: total" line should reflect the new ceiling.

If you tell me your host RAM, I can suggest a specific memory value.

Sorry, let me actually walk through it.

WSL is the Linux environment Claude Code is running in right now, inside your Windows machine. The settings file `.wslconfig` lives on the Windows side and tells WSL how much memory it can use. By default WSL takes up to half your host RAM, which is often not enough when we're running parallel agents.

# Your job

Rewrite NOTABLE_CASES.md so that a researcher coming in cold can read it as a coherent reference document, while preserving every citation and quote. Overwrite the existing file.

# Existing structure (10 top-level sections)

1. Uncommon tax-asset types
2. Non-modal payment rates
3. Up-C structures without a TRA
4. TRA preservation through corporate restructuring
5. TRA disposition in bankruptcy
6. Mechanism-2 negotiated termination payments
7. Pass-through and cross-CIK structures
8. Firms with multiple TRAs
9. Notable `never_executed` cases
10. Capital-structure oddities worth flagging

These themes are fine as the top-level grouping. The work is within-section.

# Within each section

- Cluster related entries: group entries with the same sub-pattern together, with a brief shared introductory sentence rather than repeating the pattern in each entry.
- Cut wording duplication: many entries restate the modal template ("85%, Section 754, Up-C") before naming the variation; assume the reader has the modal template in mind and lead with the variation.
- Trim filler clauses: "this is the first case of X in the dataset", "this is the cleanest example so far", and similar self-referential remarks should be cut unless they carry signal about how isolated the pattern is.
- Preserve every cited accession number, dollar amount, date, and exact-substring quote. Citations are load-bearing.
- Keep firm names and CIK references so the reader can find the source directory.
- Keep status flags (`in_force`, `terminated_by_amendment`, etc.) as terms of art; do not paraphrase them.

# Section intros

Add a one to three sentence introduction at the top of each section explaining the dimension along which entries vary. The introduction should let the reader skim section headings and know roughly what to expect inside.

# Top-of-file

Keep the existing top-of-file framing paragraph(s). Consider adding a brief table of contents linking to the 10 sections.

# Tone

Research-reference, not working log. The reader should not have to skim past "this entry was added in wave 18" or "see also above" — the file should read as a single coherent document. No closing summary section.

# Hard rules

- Banned terms: do not write the word "prose", "delve", "leverage" as a verb, "comprehensive", "robust" outside statistical context, "surface" as a verb, "dedup", "provenance", "smoke test", "triage", "EFTS". Em-dashes only in sentences with a prior comma or semicolon. Do not use the negation-correction pattern (a comma followed by "not" followed by an alternative).
- Do not introduce new factual claims. If two entries seem to contradict, leave both with their original wording and add a one-sentence note flagging the tension (rather than picking a side).
- Do not abbreviate without spelling out first.

# Sanity check before declaring done

After writing the new file, count the number of firm-entries (firms named with a brief description) and report it back to me. The current file's entry count should be approximately preserved (we are reorganizing, not pruning firms).

# Output

Write the consolidated NOTABLE_CASES.md to its existing path. Return to me: (a) the new line count, (b) the entry count, (c) a one-sentence note on any tension you couldn't reconcile (so I know what to look at), (d) whether you cut any firm-entry entirely (you should not have to).

# File

`/home/sulli/research/tra/coauthor/2026-05-12-edgar-scrape/findings/test-run/NOTABLE_CASES.md` — overwrite the existing file (use Edit, not Write).

# Context

The file collects findings from per-firm TRA-classification runs across 751 firms. It captures variations from the modal Up-C / Section 754 / 85% TRA template. It has accreted across many waves of work and now reads as a working log rather than a reference document.

# Existing structure (10 top-level sections)

1. Uncommon tax-asset types
2. Non-modal payment rates
3. Up-C structures without a TRA
4. TRA preservation through corporate restructuring
5. TRA disposition in bankruptcy
6. Mechanism-2 negotiated termination payments
7. Pass-through and cross-CIK structures
8. Firms with multiple TRAs
9. Notable `never_executed` cases
10. Capital-structure oddities worth flagging

Keep these as the top-level grouping. The work is within-section.

# Process (section-by-section)

For each of the 10 sections:

1. Read just that section from the source (use Read with offset/limit; do not load the whole file at once).
2. Compose the consolidated version of that section in your working notes.
3. Apply the change via Edit (old_string = the existing section content; new_string = the consolidated version).

The Edit call replaces one section's content with the consolidated version. The other sections stay untouched until you reach them. This keeps your working memory bounded per section.

Optional first step: Read the existing top-of-file framing (lines 1 to 5 or so) and decide whether to add a brief table of contents linking to the 10 sections. If yes, do it as a separate Edit on the head of the file.

# Within each section

- Cluster related entries: group entries with the same sub-pattern, with one brief shared introductory sentence rather than repeating the pattern in each entry.
- Cut wording duplication: many entries restate the modal template ("85%, Section 754, Up-C") before naming the variation; assume the reader has the modal template in mind and lead with the variation.
- Trim filler: "first case of X in the dataset", "cleanest example so far", and similar self-referential remarks should be cut unless they carry signal about how isolated the pattern is.
- Preserve every cited accession number, dollar amount, date, and exact-substring quote.
- Keep firm names and CIK references so the reader can locate the source directory.
- Keep status flags (`in_force`, `terminated_by_amendment`, etc.) as terms of art; do not paraphrase them.

# Section intros

Add a one to three sentence intro at the top of each section explaining the dimension along which entries vary.

# Tone

Research-reference. The reader should not see "added in wave 18" or "see also above". Read as a single coherent document. No closing summary section.

# Hard rules

- Banned terms: do not write the word "prose", "delve", "leverage" as a verb, "comprehensive", "robust" outside statistical context, "surface" as a verb, "dedup", "provenance", "smoke test", "triage", "EFTS". Em-dashes only in sentences with a prior comma or semicolon. Do not use the negation-correction pattern (a comma followed by "not" followed by an alternative).
- Do not introduce new factual claims. If two entries seem to contradict, leave both with original wording and add a one-sentence note flagging the tension.
- Do not abbreviate without spelling out first.
